Mirroring Call-by-Need, or Values Acting Silly
Beniamino Accattoli, Adrienne Lancelot

Abstract
Call-by-need evaluation for the λ \lambda -calculus can be seen as merging the best of call-by-name and call-by-value, namely the wise erasing behaviour of the former and the wise duplicating behaviour of the latter. To better understand how duplication and erasure can be combined, we design a degenerated calculus, dubbed call-by-silly , that is symmetric to call-by-need in that it merges the worst of call-by-name and call-by-value, namely silly duplications by-name and silly erasures by-value. We validate the design of the call-by-silly calculus via rewriting properties and multi types. In particular, we mirror the main theorem about call-by-need—that is, its operational equivalence with call-by-name—showing that call-by-silly and call-by-value induce the same contextual equivalence. This fact shows the blindness with respect to efficiency of call-by-value contextual equivalence. We also define a call-by-silly strategy and measure its length via tight multi types. Lastly, we prove that the call-by-silly strategy computes evaluation sequences of maximal length in the calculus.

1
Introduction

Plotkin’s call-by-value and Wadsworth’s call-by-need (untyped)
\lambda
-calculi were introduced in the 1970s as more application-oriented variants of the ordinary call-by-name
\lambda
-calculus
[
53
,
49
]
. The simpler call-by-value (shortened to CbV) calculus has found a logical foundation in formalisms related to classical logic or linear logic
[
28
,
44
,
45
,
33
]
.

The foundation of call-by-need (CbNeed) is less developed, particularly its logical interpretation. The duality related to classical logic can accommodate CbNeed, as shown by Ariola et al.
[
19
,
17
]
, but it does not provide an explanation for it. Within linear logic, CbNeed is understood as a sort of
affine
CbV, according to Maraist et al.
[
47
]
. Such an interpretation however is unusual, because it does not match exactly with cut-elimination in linear logic, as for call-by-name (CbN) and CbV, and it is rather connected with
affine logic
.

CbNeed Optimizes CbN.

The main foundational theorem for CbNeed is the
operational equivalence of CbN and CbNeed
due to Ariola et al.
[
18
]
, that is, the fact that they induce the same contextual equivalence, despite being based on different evaluation mechanisms. The result formalizes that CbNeed is a semantic-preserving optimization of CbN.

An elegant semantic proof of this result is given by Kesner
[
38
]
. She shows that the CbN multi type system by de Carvalho
[
30
,
31
]
(considered before his seminal work also by Gardner
[
35
]
and Kfoury
[
43
]
), which characterizes termination for CbN (that is,
t
is typable
\Leftrightarrow

t
is CbN terminating), characterizes termination also for CbNeed. Multi types are also known as
non-idempotent intersection types
, and have strong ties with linear logic.

Duplication and Erasure, Wise and Silly.

The linear logic interpretation of CbN and CbV provides some operational insights about CbNeed. By bringing to the fore duplication and erasure, linear logic underlines a second symmetry between CbN and CbV, which is independent of classical logic as it can already be observed in an intuitionistic setting. The idea is that CbN is
wise
with respect to erasure, because it never evaluates arguments that might be erased (for instance
(\lambda x.\mathtt{I})\Omega\rightarrow_{CbN}\mathtt{I}
, where
\mathtt{I}
is the identity and
\Omega
is the looping combinator), while it is
silly
with respect to duplications, as it repeats possibly many times the evaluation of arguments that are used at least once (for instance,
(\lambda x.xx)(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})\rightarrow_{CbN}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})
). Symmetrically, CbV is silly with respect to erasures, as it reduces in arguments that are not going to be used (for instance looping on
(\lambda x.\mathtt{I})\Omega
), but it is wise for duplications, as it reduces only once arguments that shall be used at least once (e.g.
(\lambda x.xx)(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})\rightarrow_{{CbV}}(\lambda x.xx)\mathtt{I}
).

CbNeed is Pure Wiseness.

In this framework, one can see CbNeed as merging the best of CbN and CbV, i.e. wise erasure and wise duplication. In
[
12
]
, this insight guides Accattoli et al. in extending Kesner’s above-mentioned study of CbNeed via multi types
[
38
]
. Starting from existing multi type systems for CbN and CbV, they pick out the aspects for CbN wise erasures and CbV wise duplication as to build a CbNeed multi type system. Their system characterizes CbNeed termination, as does the CbN system in
[
38
]
. Additionally, it tightly characterizes CbNeed evaluation
quantitatively
: from type derivations one can read the
exact
number of CbNeed evaluation steps, which is not possible in the CbN system.

Pure Silliness.

This paper studies an unusual—and at first counter-intuitive—combination of duplication and erasure. The idea is to mix together silly erasure and silly duplication, completing the following diagram of strategies with its new
call-by-silly
(CbS) corner:

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{CbS}}
Silly erasure
Silly duplication
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{CbN}}
Wise erasure
Silly duplication
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{CbV}}
Silly erasure
Wise duplication
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{CbNeed}}
Wise erasure
Wise duplication
Duplication
Erasure
Duplication
Erasure

(1)

Designing the CbS calculus and a CbS evaluation strategy is of no interest for programming purposes, as CbS is desperately inefficient, by construction. It is theoretically relevant, however, because it showcases how modularly duplication and erasure can be combined. As we shall discuss, the study of CbS also contributes to the understanding of CbV.

Quantitative Goal and Micro Steps.

We introduce CbS and provide evidence of its good design via contributions that mirror as much as possible the theory of CbNeed. Our ultimate test is to mirror the quantitative study of CbNeed by Accattoli et al.
[
12
]
, via a new system of multi types for CbS. It is a challenging design goal because this kind of quantitative results requires a
perfect matching
between the operational and the multi types semantics.

As we explain at the end of Sect.
9
, such a goal does not seem to be cleanly achievable within the
\lambda
-calculus, where duplication is
small-step
, that is, arguments are substituted on
all
the occurrences of a variable at once. It is instead attainable in a
micro-step
setting with explicit substitutions, where one copy at a time is performed. Moreover, in the
\lambda
-calculus duplication and erasure are hard to disentangle as they are both handled by
\beta
-reduction.

Our Framework.

A good setting for our purpose is Accattoli and Kesner’s
linear substitution calculus
(LSC)
[
1
,
8
]
, a calculus with explicit substitutions which is a variation over a calculus by Milner
[
48
,
39
]
exploiting ideas from another work of theirs
[
13
]
. A key feature of the LSC is that it has
separate
rules for duplication and erasure, and
just one rule for each
.

The LSC comes in two dialects, CbN and CbV, designed over linear logic proof nets
[
3
,
2
]
. The further CbNeed LSC is obtained by taking the duplication rule of the CbV LSC and the erasing rule of the CbN LSC. It was first defined by Accattoli et al.
[
5
]
and then studied or extended by many recent works on CbNeed
[
38
,
21
,
6
,
7
,
41
,
23
,
12
,
40
,
22
,
15
]
.

Our starting point is the principled definition of the new
silly (linear) substitution calculus
(SSC), the variant of the LSC obtained by mirroring the construction for CbNeed, namely by putting together the CbN rule for duplication and the CbV one for erasure. In this way, all the corners of the strategy diagram (
1
) fit into the same framework. Since CbNeed is usually studied with respect to weak evaluation (that is, not under abstraction), we only consider weak evaluation for the SSC (and leave the strong, unrestricted case to future work).

Contribution 1: Rewriting Properties.

After defining the SSC, we prove some of the rewriting properties that are expected from every well-behaved calculus. Namely, we provide a characterization of its normal forms, that reduction is confluent, and that—as it is the case for all dialects of the LSC—the CbV erasing rule can be postponed.

Via the multi types of the next contribution, we also prove
uniform normalization
for the SSC, that is, the fact that a term is weakly normalizing (i.e. it reduces to a normal form) if and only if it is strongly normalizing (i.e. it has no diverging reductions). This is trivially true in (weak) CbV, where diverging sub-terms cannot be erased and terms with redexes cannot be duplicated, and false in CbN and CbNeed, where diverging sub-terms can be erased. In the SSC, it is true but non-trivial, because terms with redexes can be duplicated.

Contribution 2: Operational Equivalence.

Next, we develop the mirrored image of the main qualitative result for CbNeed, that is, we prove the operational equivalence of CbS and CbV. We do so by mirroring Kesner’s semantic proof via multi types
[
38
]
.

To this purpose, we cannot use the existing multi type system for CbV, due to Ehrhard
[
32
]
, as Kesner’s proof is based on a system for the
slower
of the two systems, in her case CbN. Therefore, we introduce a silly multi type system, designed in a dual way to the one for CbNeed by Accattoli et al.
[
12
]
.

Our system is a minor variant over a system for CbN strong normalization by Kesner and Ventura
[
42
]
, and it also bears strong similarities with a system for CbV by Manzonetto et al.
[
46
,
37
]
. We prove that it characterizes termination for closed terms in both CbV and CbS, from which it follows the coincidence of their respective contextual equivalences. This result shows that CbV is a semantic-preserving optimization of CbS, exactly as CbNeed is for CbN.

CbV Contextual Equivalence is Blind to Efficiency.

Contextual equivalence is usually considered as
the
notion of program equivalence.
This paper points out a fundamental limitation of contextual equivalence for the effect-free untyped CbV
\lambda
-calculus. In absence of effects, contextual equivalence does distinguish between CbV and CbN because of their different erasing policies (as they change the notion of termination) but it is unable to distinguish between their duplication policies (which only impact the efficiency of evaluation), because it is
blind to efficiency
: it cannot ’count’ how many times a sub-term is evaluated.

It is well known that, in richer frameworks with effects, CbV contextual equivalence can ’count’. In presence of state, indeed, more terms are discriminated, as doing once
or
twice the same operation can now change the content of a state and contexts are expressive enough to generate and distinguish these state changes
[
52
,
25
]
. But it is instead not well known, or not fully digested, that in the pure setting this is not possible—thus that CbV contextual equivalence validates silly duplications—as we have repeatedly noted in discussions with surprised colleagues.

To the best of our knowledge, such a blindness to efficiency has not been properly established before. Proving contextual equivalence is technically difficult, often leading to hand-waving arguments. Moreover, figuring out a general characterization of silly duplications is tricky. We provide here an easy way out to prove CbV contextual equivalence for terms related by silly duplications: it is enough to reduce them to the same normal form in the SSC, a calculus qualitatively equivalent to CbV but based on silly duplications.

Note also that the equivalence of CbN and CbNeed is interpreted positively (despite implying that CbNeed contextual equivalence is blind to efficiency) because it gives a foundation to CbNeed. Curiously, the mirrored equivalence of CbV and CbS crystallizes instead a negative fact about pure CbV, since CbV is expected to be efficiency-sensitive.

Contribution 3: The CbS Strategy.

Beyond the SSC, we specify a tricky notion of CbS
strategy
. CbS contexts are the modification of CbN contexts that also evaluate shared sub-terms when they are
no longer needed
, i.e., after all the needed copies have already been evaluated. The CbS strategy is an extension of the CbN strategy, on non-erasing steps.

We then prove that the CbS strategy is (essentially) deterministic (precisely, it is
diamond
, a weakened form of determinism), and that the CbV erasing rule of the strategy can be postponed. These usually simple results have in our case simple but lengthy proofs, because of the tricky grammar defining CbS contexts.

Contribution 4: Tight Types and (Maximal) Evaluation Lengths.

Lastly, we show that the quantitative result for CbNeed by Accattoli et al.
[
12
]
is also mirrored by our silly type system. We mimic
[
12
]
and we extract the exact length of evaluations for the CbS strategy from a notion of
tight
type derivation.

We then use this quantitative result to show that the CbS strategy actually computes a
maximal
evaluation sequence in the weak SSC. This is not so surprising, given that maximal evaluations and strong normalization are related and measured with similar systems of multi types, as in Bernadet and Lengrand
[
24
]
, Kesner and Ventura
[
42
]
, and Accattoli et al.
[
9
]
.

At the same time, however, the maximality of CbS is specific to weak evaluation and different from those results in the literature, which concern strong evaluation (that is, also under abstraction). In particular, CbS would
not
be maximal in a strong setting (which we do not treat here), as it would erase a value before evaluating it (as CbV also does). This is precisely the difference between our type system and that of Kesner and Ventura.

Related Work.

The only work that might be vaguely reminiscent of ours is by Ariola et al.
[
19
,
17
]
, who study CbNeed with respect to the classical duality between CbN and CbV and control operators. Their work and ours however are orthogonal and incomparable: they do not obtain inefficient strategies and we do not deal with control operators.

Proofs.

Most proofs are in the Appendix.

2
The Weak Silly Substitution Calculus

​​​​​​​​​

\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}\textsc{Language}&&&\textsc{Root rules}\\[2.0pt]
\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{0.3cm}}rll}\textsc{Terms}\hskip 8.5359pt&t,s,u&::=&x\mid\lambda x.t\mid ts\mid t[x{\shortleftarrow}s]\\
\textsc{Values}\hskip 8.5359pt&v,v^{\prime}&::=&\lambda x.t\\
\textsc{Sub. Ctxs}\hskip 8.5359pt&S,{S^{\prime}}&::=&\langle\cdot\rangle\mid S[x{\shortleftarrow}s]\end{array}&&&\begin{array}[]{rll}S\langle\lambda x.t\rangle s&\mapsto_{\mathtt{m}}&S\langle t[x{\shortleftarrow}s]\rangle\\
W\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}[x{\shortleftarrow}s]&\mapsto_{\mathtt{e}_{W}}&W\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}s\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}[x{\shortleftarrow}s]\\
t[x{\shortleftarrow}S\langle v\rangle]&\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}&S\langle t\rangle\ \ \ \mbox{if }x\notin\mathtt{fv}(t)\end{array}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c@{\hspace{0.5cm}}c}\lx@intercol\hfil\textsc{Weak reduction }\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[4.0pt]

W\hskip 4.26773pt&::=\hskip 2.84544pt&\langle\cdot\rangle\mid Wt\mid tW\mid t[x{\shortleftarrow}W]\mid W[x{\shortleftarrow}s]\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt\end{array}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\begin{array}[]{rcl}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}&:=&W\langle\mapsto_{\mathtt{m}}\rangle\\
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}&:=&W\langle\mapsto_{\mathtt{e}_{W}}\rangle\\
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}&:=&W\langle\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}\rangle\\
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}&:=&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 1:
The (weak) silly (linear) substitution calculus (SSC).

Inception.

The SSC is a variant over Accattoli and Kesner’s linear substitution calculus (LSC)
[
1
,
8
]
, which is a micro-step
\lambda
-calculus with explicit substitutions.
Micro-step
means that substitutions act on one variable occurrence at a time, rather than
small-step
, that is, on all occurrences at the same time. The LSC exists in two main variants, CbN and CbV. The CbV variant is usually presented with small-step rules, and called
value substitution calculus
, the micro-step (or linear) variant of which appears for instance in Accattoli et al.
[
5
,
12
]
. The two calculi have similar and yet different duplication and erasing rewriting rules.
We refer to
[
5
,
12
]
for a uniform presentation of CbN, CbV, and CbNeed in the LSC.

The SSC in Fig.
1
is obtained dually to CbNeed, taking the duplication rule
\mapsto_{\mathtt{e}_{W}}
of the CbN LSC and the erasing rule
\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
of the CbV LSC. Its evaluation is micro-step. We only define its weak evaluation, which does not enter into abstractions, as it is often done in comparative studies between CbN, CbV, and CbNeed such as
[
5
,
12
]
. This sections overviews the definitions in the figure. The SSC deals with possibly open terms. The CbS strategy, to be defined in Sect.
8
, shall instead deal only with closed terms.

Terms.

Terms of the SSC are the same as for the LSC and extend the
\lambda
-calculus with
explicit substitutions

t[x{\shortleftarrow}s]
(shortened to ESs), that is
a more compact notation for
{\sf let}\ x=s\ {\sf in}\ t
, but where the order of evaluation between
t
and
s
is a priori not fixed.
The set
\mathtt{fv}(t)
of
free
variables of a term
t
is defined as expected, in particular,

\mathtt{fv}(t[x{\shortleftarrow}s]):=(\mathtt{fv}(t)\setminus\{x\})\cup\mathtt{fv}(s)
. Both
\lambda x.t
and
t[x{\shortleftarrow}s]
bind
x
in
t
, and terms are considered up to
\alpha
-renaming.
A term
t
is
closed
if
\mathtt{fv}(t)=\emptyset
,
open
otherwise.
As usual, terms are identified up to
\alpha
-equivalence. Meta-level capture-avoiding substitution is noted
t\{x{\shortleftarrow}s\}
. Note that values are only abstraction; this choice shall be motivated in the next section.

Contexts.

Contexts are terms with exactly one occurrence of the
hole

\langle\cdot\rangle
, an additional constant, standing for a removed sub-term. We shall use many different contexts. The most general ones in this paper are
weak contexts

W
, which simply allow the hole to be anywhere but under abstraction. To define the rewriting rules,
substitution contexts

S
(i.e. lists of explicit substitutions) also play a role.
The main operation about contexts is
plugging

W\langle t\rangle
where the hole
\langle\cdot\rangle
in context

W
is replaced by
t
. Plugging, as usual with contexts, can
capture variables—for instance
((\langle\cdot\rangle t)[x{\shortleftarrow}s])\langle x\rangle=(xt)[x{\shortleftarrow}s]
.
We write
W\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}t\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
when we want to stress that the context
W
does not capture the free variables of
t
.

Rewriting Rules.

The reduction rules of the SSC are slightly unusual as they use
contexts
both to allow one to reduce redexes located in sub-terms, which is standard,
and
to define the redexes themselves, which is less standard. This approach is called
at a distance
and related to cut-elimination on proof nets (from which the terminology
multiplicative
and
exponential
to be discussed next is also taken), see Accattoli
[
2
,
3
]
.

The
multiplicative rule

\mapsto_{\mathtt{m}}
is essentially the
\beta
-rule, except that the argument goes into a new ES, rather then being immediately substituted, and that there can be a substitution context
S
in between the abstraction and the argument. Example:
(\lambda x.y)[y{\shortleftarrow}t]s\mapsto_{\mathtt{m}}y[x{\shortleftarrow}s][y{\shortleftarrow}t]
. One with on-the-fly
\alpha
-renaming is
(\lambda x.y)[y{\shortleftarrow}t]y\mapsto_{\mathtt{m}}z[x{\shortleftarrow}y][z{\shortleftarrow}t]
.

The
exponential rule

\mapsto_{\mathtt{e}_{W}}
replaces a single variable occurrence, the one appearing in the context
W
. Example:
(xx)[x{\shortleftarrow}y[y{\shortleftarrow}t]]\mapsto_{\mathtt{e}_{W}}(x(y[y{\shortleftarrow}t]))[x{\shortleftarrow}y[y{\shortleftarrow}t]]
. The notation
\mapsto_{\mathtt{e}_{W}}
stresses that the rule is parametric in a notion of context
W
, that specifies where the variable replacements are allowed, and which shall be exploited to define the CbS strategy in Sect.
8
.

The
garbage collection (GC) rule by value

\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
eliminates a value
v
, keeping the list of substitutions
S
previously surrounding the value, which might contain terms that are not values, and so cannot be erased with
v
. This rule is the CbV erasing rule. Example:
(\lambda z.yy)[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}[w{\shortleftarrow}t]]\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}(\lambda z.yy)[w{\shortleftarrow}t]
, where
\mathtt{I}
is the identity.

The three root rules
\mapsto_{\mathtt{m}}
,
\mapsto_{\mathtt{e}_{W}}
, and
\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
are then closed by weak contexts. We shorten
W\langle t\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}W\langle s\rangle
if
t\mapsto_{\mathtt{m}}s
with
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}:=W\langle\mapsto_{\mathtt{m}}\rangle
, and similarly for the other rules. The reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
encompasses all possible reductions in the weak SSC.

Silliness Check and the Silly Extra Copy.

Let us evaluate with the SSC the examples used in the introduction to explain silly and wise behaviour. About silly erasures,
(\lambda x.\mathtt{I})\Omega
diverges, as in CbV. One can reduce it to
\mathtt{I}[x{\shortleftarrow}\Omega]
, but then there is no way of erasing
\Omega
, which is not a value and does not reduce to a value. About silly duplications,
(\lambda x.xx)(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})
can reduce both to
\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})
and to
(\lambda x.xx)\mathtt{I}
, as in CbN. The silly aspect is the fact that one
can
reduce to
\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})
, since in CbV this is
not
possible. The CbS strategy of Sect.
8
shall always select the silly option.

Note that
(\lambda x.xx)(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})
reduces to
(xx)[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]
and then
three
copies of
\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}
can be evaluated, as one can reduce to
\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]
and the copy in the ES has to be evaluated before being erased, because it is not a value. Such a further copy would not be evaluated in CbN, as terms are erased without being evaluated. We refer to this aspect as to the
silly extra copy
.

3
Basic Rewriting Notions

Given a rewriting relation
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}
, we write
d\colon t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}s
for a
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}
-reduction sequence from
t
to
s
, the length of which is noted
|d|
. Moreover, we use
|d|_{a}
for the number of
a
-
steps
in
d
, for a sub-relation
\rightarrow_{a}
of
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}
.

A term
t
is
weakly
\mathsf{r}
-normalizing
, noted
t\in\mbox{WN}_{\mathsf{r}}
, if
d\colon t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}s
with
s

\mathsf{r}
-normal; and
t
is
strongly
\mathsf{r}
-normalizing
, noted
t\in\mbox{SN}_{\mathsf{r}}
, if there are no diverging
\mathsf{r}
-sequences from
t
, or, equivalently, if all its reducts are in
\mbox{SN}_{\mathsf{r}}
.

According to Dal Lago and Martini
[
29
]
, a relation
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}
is
diamond
if
s_{1}\,{}_{\mathsf{r}}\!\!\prescript{}{}{\leftarrow}t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}s_{2}
and
s_{1}\neq s_{2}
imply
s_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}u\,{}_{\mathsf{r}}\!\!\prescript{}{}{\leftarrow}s_{2}
for some
u
.
If
\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}
is diamond then:

1.

Confluence
:

\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}
is confluent, that is,
s_{1}\,{}_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}\!\!\prescript{}{}{\leftarrow}t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}s_{2}
implies
s_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}u\,{}_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}\!\!\prescript{}{}{\leftarrow}s_{2}
for some
u
;

2.

Length invariance
: all
\mathsf{r}
-evaluations to normal form with the same start term have the same length (i.e. if
d\colon t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}s
and
d^{\prime}\colon t\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}^{*}s
with
s

\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}
-normal then
|d|=|d^{\prime}|
);

3.

Uniform normalization
:
t
is weakly
\mathsf{r}
-normalizing if and only if it is strongly
\mathsf{r}
-normalizing.

Basically, the diamond captures a more liberal form of determinism.

4
Rewriting Properties

In this section, we study some rewriting properties of the weak SSC. We give a characterization of weak normal forms and prove postponement of garbage collection by value and confluence.

Characterization of Normal Forms.

The characterization of (possibly open) weak normal forms requires the following concept.

Definition 1

(Shallow free variables)
.

The set
\mathtt{shfv}(t)
of
shallow free variables
of
t
is the set of variables with occurrences out of abstractions in
t
:

\begin{array}[]{lll @{\hspace{.5cm}} lll}\mathtt{shfv}(x)&:=&\{x\}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\mathtt{shfv}(\lambda x.t)&:=&\emptyset\\
\mathtt{shfv}(ts)&:=&\mathtt{shfv}(t)\cup\mathtt{shfv}(s)\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&\mathtt{shfv}(t[x{\shortleftarrow}s])&:=&(\mathtt{shfv}(t)\setminus\{x\})\cup\mathtt{shfv}(s)\end{array}

{toappendix}

Proposition 2
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
A

t
is
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
-normal if and only if
t
is a weak normal term according to the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{.5cm}}r@{\hspace{.2cm}}l@{\hspace{.3cm}}lll}\textsc{Weak answers}\hskip 14.22636pt&a,a\hskip 5.69046pt&::=\hfil\hskip 8.5359pt&v&\lx@intercol\mid a[x{\shortleftarrow}i]\mbox{ with }x\notin\mathtt{shfv}(a)\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hskip 14.22636pt&\hskip 5.69046pt&\hfil\hskip 8.5359pt&&\lx@intercol\mid a[x{\shortleftarrow}a^{\prime}]\mbox{ with }x\in\mathtt{fv}(a)\setminus\mathtt{shfv}(a)\hfil\lx@intercol\\[4.0pt]
\textsc{Inert terms}\hskip 14.22636pt&i,i^{\prime}\hskip 5.69046pt&::=\hfil\hskip 8.5359pt&x&\mid in&\mid i[x{\shortleftarrow}i^{\prime}]\mbox{ with }x\notin\mathtt{shfv}(i)\\
\hskip 14.22636pt&\hskip 5.69046pt&\hfil\hskip 8.5359pt&&&\mid i[x{\shortleftarrow}a]\mbox{ with }x\in\mathtt{fv}(i)\setminus\mathtt{shfv}(i)\\[0.0pt]
\textsc{Weak normal terms}\hskip 14.22636pt&n,n^{\prime}\hskip 5.69046pt&::=\hfil\hskip 8.5359pt&a&\mid i\end{array}

Postponement of GC by Value.

As it is usually the case in all the dialects of the LSC, the erasing rule of the weak SSC, that is
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
, can be postponed. Let
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}:=\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
.

{toappendix}

Proposition 3

(Postponement of garbage collection by value)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
A

If
d:t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}^{*}s
then
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}^{k}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}^{h}s
with
k=|d|_{\mathtt{w}\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
and
h\geq|d|_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
.

Local Termination.

To prepare for confluence, we recall the following crucial property, which is essentially inherited from the LSC, given that termination for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
is trivial.

{toappendix}

Proposition 4

(Local termination)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
A

Reductions
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}
,
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
, and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
are strongly normalizing separately.

Confluence.

The proof of confluence for the weak SSC given here is a minor variant of what would be done for the LSC, except that there is no direct proof of confluence in the literature for the LSC
1
1

1

Confluence of the LSC holds, as it follows from stronger results about residuals in Accattoli et al.
[
8
]
but here we want to avoid the heaviness of residuals.
. Overviewing the proof allows us to explain a design choice of the SSC.

The proof is based on an elegant technique resting on local diagrams and local termination, namely the Hindley-Rosen method. In our case, it amounts to prove that the three rules
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}
,
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
, and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
are confluent separately, proved by local termination and Newman’s lemma, and commute, proved by local termination and Hindley’s strong commutation. Confluence then follows by Hindley-Rosen lemma, for which the union of confluent and commuting reductions is confluent. The Hindley-Rosen method is a modular technique often used for confluence of extensions of the
\lambda
-calculus, for instance in
[
20
,
34
,
51
,
27
,
50
,
26
,
18
,
16
,
4
]
.

{toappendix}

Lemma 5

(Local confluence)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
A.2

Reductions
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
are diamond,
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
is locally confluent.

In contrast to confluence, commutation of two reductions
\rightarrow_{1}
and
\rightarrow_{2}
does
not
follow from their
local
commutation and strong normalization. In our case, however, the rules verify a non-erasing form of Hindley’s strong (local) commutation
[
36
]
of
\rightarrow_{1}
over
\rightarrow_{2}
, here dubbed
strict strong commutation
: if
s_{1}\prescript{}{1}{\leftarrow}t\rightarrow_{2}s_{2}
then
\exists u
such that
s_{1}\rightarrow_{2}^{+}u\prescript{}{1}{\leftarrow}s_{2}
, that is, on one side of the commutation there are no duplications of steps, and on both sides there are no erasures, because
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
can only erase values, which do not contain redexes since evaluation is weak. Strong commutation and strong normalization do imply commutation.

{toappendix}

Lemma 6

(Strict strong local commutations)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
A.2

Reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
(resp.
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
; resp.
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
) strictly strongly commutes over
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}
(resp.
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
; resp.
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}
).

Theorem 7

(Confluence)
.

Reductions
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
are confluent.

Proof.

By Newman lemma, local confluence (Lemma
5
) and local termination (Prop.
4
) imply that
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}
,
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
, and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
are confluent separately. By a result of Hindley
[
36
]
, strict strong local commutation (Lemma
6
) and local termination imply that
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}
,
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
, and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
are pairwise commuting. By Hindley-Rosen lemma,
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}=\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}=\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
are confluent.∎

Remark 8
.

The design choice that values are only abstraction is motivated by the commutation of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
over
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
. Indeed, if one considers variables as values (thus allowing the erasure of variables by
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
), such a commutation fails, and confluence does not hold, as the following non-commuting (and non-confluent) span shows:
x[z{\shortleftarrow}ww]\prescript{}{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}{\leftarrow}x[y{\shortleftarrow}z][z{\shortleftarrow}ww]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}_{W}}x[y{\shortleftarrow}ww][z{\shortleftarrow}ww]
.

Interestingly, something similar happens in CbNeed, where values are only abstractions too. Indeed, if one considers variables as values (thus allowing the duplication of variables in CbNeed), then one has the following non-closable critical pair for the CbNeed strategy, non-closable because
y
is not needed in
\mathtt{I}(\lambda z.wx)[x{\shortleftarrow}y][y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]
(the diagram closes in the CbNeed
calculus
but the CbNeed
strategy
is not confluent):
x(\lambda z.wx)[x{\shortleftarrow}y][y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]
x(\lambda z.wx)[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]
\mathtt{I}(\lambda z.wx)[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]
y(\lambda z.wx)[x{\shortleftarrow}y][y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]
\mathtt{I}(\lambda z.wx)[x{\shortleftarrow}y][y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]

5
Silly Multi Types

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\begin{array}[]{rrcll}\textsc{Linear types}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{}^{\prime}}}&::=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{M}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}\\
\textsc{Multi types}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{M}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{N}}&::=&{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}_{i\in I}\ \ \mbox{where $I$ is a finite set}\\
\textsc{Generic types}&{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{T}},{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{{}^{\prime}}}&::=&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{M}}\end{array}\\[20.0pt]

Figure 2:
The silly multi type system.

In this section, we introduce multi types and the silly multi type system.

Inception.

The design of the silly type system is specular to the one for CbNeed by Accattoli et al.
[
12
]
. The CbNeed one tweaks the CbV system in the literature (due to Ehrhard
[
32
]
) by changing its rules for applications and ESs, which are the rules using multi-sets, as to accommodate CbN erasures. The silly one given here tweaks the CbN system in the literature (due to de Carvalho
[
31
]
) by changing the same rules to accommodate CbV erasures. In both cases, the underlying system is responsible for the duplication behavior. The desired erasure behavior is then enforced by changing the rules using multi-sets.

The silly system is in Fig.
2
. It is the variant of the system for CbN in
[
12
]
(itself a reformulation of
[
31
]
tuned for weak evaluation) obtained by adding ’
\uplus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}
’ in the right premise of both rules
@
and
\mathsf{ES}
. Such an extra typing for these rules captures the
silly extra copy
mentioned at the end of Sect.
2
. More details are given at the end of this section.

CbS Types and Judgements.

Linear types
and
multi(-sets) types
are defined by mutual induction in Fig.
2
.
Note the linear constant
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}
used to type abstractions, which are normal terms. For conciseness, sometimes we shorten it to
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}}
. We shall show that every normalizing term is typable with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}
, hence its name. The constant
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}
shall also play a role in our quantitative study in Sect.
9
. The empty multi set
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}
is also noted
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbf{0}}}
.

A multi type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}_{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}
has to be intended as a conjunction
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}_{1}\land\dots\land{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}_{n}
of linear types
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}_{n}
, for a commutative, associative, non-idempotent conjunction
\land
(morally a tensor
\otimes
), of neutral element
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbf{0}}}
.
The intuition is that a linear type corresponds to a single use of a term
t
, and that
t
is typed with a
multiset

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{M}}
of
n
linear types if it is going to be used (at most)
n
times, that is, if
t
is part of a larger term
s
, then a copy
of
t
shall end up in evaluation position during the evaluation of
s
.

Judgments
have shape
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}\vdash^{(m,e)}t\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{T}}
where
t
is a term,
m
and
e
are two natural numbers,
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{T}}
is either a multi type or a
linear type, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}
is a
type context
, i.e., a total function from variables to multi types
such that
\mathtt{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}):=\{x\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}(x)\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbf{0}}}\}
is finite, usually written as
x_{1}\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{M}}_{1},\dots,x_{n}\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{M}}_{n}
(with
n\in{\rm Nature}
) if
\mathtt{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}})\subseteq\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}(x_{i})={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{M}}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
.

The indices
m
and
e
shall be used for measuring the length of evaluation sequences via type derivations, namely to measure the number of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
steps. There is no index for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
steps in order to stay close to the type systems in Accattoli et al.
[
12
]
for CbN/CbV/CbNeed, that do not have an index for GC either; the reason being that GC (by value) can be postponed. A quick look to the typing rules shows that
m
and
e
are not really
needed, as
m
can be recovered as the number of
\mathtt{app}
rules, and
e
as the number of
\mathtt{ax}
rules. It is however handy to note them explicitly.

We write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}\vdash t\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{T}}
when the information given by
m
and
e
is not relevant.

Typing Rules.

The abstraction rule
\lambda
uses the notation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}\setminus\!\!\!\setminus x}
for the type context defined as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}
on every variable but possibly
x
, for which
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}\setminus\!\!\!\setminus x)(x)={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbf{0}}}
. It is a compact way to express the rule in both the cases
x\in\mathtt{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}})
and
x\notin\mathtt{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}})
.

Rules
@
and
\mathsf{ES}
require the argument to be typed with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{M}}\uplus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}
, which is necessarily introduced by rule
\mathsf{many}
, the hypotheses of which are a multi set of derivations, indexed by a possibly empty set
I
. When
I
is empty, the rule has one premises, the one for
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}
.

Type Derivations.

We write
\pi\,\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}\vdash t\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}
if
\pi
is a (
type
)
derivation
(i.e. a tree constructed using the rules in Fig.
2
) of final judgment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}\vdash t\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}
.

The
size

|\pi|
of a derivation
\pi\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(m,e)}{t}\ {:}\ {A}
is the number of non-
\mathsf{many}
rules, which is always greater or equal to the sum of the indices, that is,
|\pi|\geq m+e
.

Further Technicalities about Types.

The type context
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}
is
empty
if
\mathtt{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}})=\emptyset
, and we write
\vdash t\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}
when
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}
is empty.

Multi-set sum

\uplus
is extended to type contexts point-wise,
i.e.
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}\uplus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Delta}})(x):={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}(x)\uplus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Delta}}(x)
for each variable
x
.
This notion is extended to a finite family of type contexts as expected,
in particular
\uplus_{i\in J\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}_{i}
is the empty context when
J=\emptyset
.
Given two type contexts
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Delta}}
such that
\mathtt{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}})\cap\mathtt{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Delta}})=\emptyset
, the
type
context
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Delta}}
is defined by
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Delta}})(x):={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}(x)
if
x\in\mathtt{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}})
,
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Delta}})(x):={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Delta}}(x)
if
x\in\mathtt{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Delta}})
, and
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Delta}})(x):={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbf{0}}}
otherwise.
Note that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}},x\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbf{0}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}
, where we implicitly assume
x\notin\mathtt{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}})
.

Relevance.

Note that no weakening is allowed in axioms. An easy induction then shows:

{toappendix}

Lemma 9

(Type contexts and variable occurrences)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
B

Let
\pi\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(m,e)}{t}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}
be a derivation. Then
\mathtt{shfv}(t)\subseteq\mathtt{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}})\subseteq\mathtt{fv}(t)
.

Lemma
9
implies that derivations of closed terms have empty type context.
Note that free variables of
t
might not be in
\mathtt{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}})
,
if they only occurr in abstractions typed with
\mathsf{ax}_{\lambda}
.

Typing the Silly Extra Copy.

The empty multi type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbf{0}}}
is the type for variables that do not occur or whose occurrences are unreachable by weak evaluation. A typical example is
\lambda x.y
, that can be typed only with arrow types of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbf{0}}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}
(plus of course with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}
), because of Lemma
9
. Note that in the silly system every term—even diverging ones—can be typed with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbf{0}}}
by rule
\mathsf{many}
(taking 0 premises). In CbN, an argument for
\lambda x.y
would only need to be typed with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbf{0}}}
, as typability with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbf{0}}}
means that the term shall be erased. In the silly system, instead, the application rule
@
requires an argument of
\lambda x.y
to additionally be typed with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}
, because of the ’
\uplus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}
’ requirement for arguments (and ESs), forcing the argument to be
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
normalizing and capturing silly erasures.

Now, note that the modification
\uplus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}
at work in the silly system concerns
all
arguments and ESs, not only those associated to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbf{0}}}
. This is what correspond at the type level to the reduction of the
silly extra copy
of
every
argument/ES (out of abstractions).

Relationship with the Literature.

Our system is essentially the one used by Kesner and Ventura to measure CbN strong normalization in the LSC with respect to strong evaluation, i.e. possibly under abstraction
[
42
]
. There are two differences. Firstly, they tweak the
@
and
\mathsf{ES}
CbN rules with
\uplus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}
rather than with
\uplus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}
, that is, they allow an arbitrary linear type for the additional copy to be evaluated. Secondly, they do not have rule
\mathsf{ax}_{\lambda}
, because their evaluation is strong. Thus,
\mathtt{I}[x{\shortleftarrow}\lambda y.\Omega]
is not typable in
[
42
]
, while here it is.

The CbV system by Manzonetto et al.
[
46
]
is similar to ours in that it tweaks the CbN system and requires multi-sets to be non-empty.

6
The Weak Calculus, Types, and Strong Normalization

Here, we show that silly multi types characterize strong normalization in the weak SSC. The proof technique is standard: we prove correctness—i.e.
t
typable implies
t\in\mbox{SN}_{\mathtt{w}}
—via subject reduction, and completeness—i.e.
t\in\mbox{SN}_{\mathtt{w}}
implies
t
typable—via subject expansion and typability of normal forms.
Note that SN in our weak case is simpler than SN in strong calculi, since here erasing steps cannot erase divergence. Actually, they cannot erase
any
step, as guaranteed by strict commutation in Sect.
4
. At the end of the section, we shall indeed obtain
uniform normalization
, i.e. that weak and strong normalization for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
coincide.

Correctness.

As it is standard, subject reduction for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
is based on a linear substitution lemma, in the Appendix (Lemma
39
, page
39
).

{toappendix}

Proposition 10

(Quantitative subject reduction for Weak SSC)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
C

Let
\pi\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(m,e)}{t}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}
be a derivation.

1.

Multiplicative
: if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}s
then
m\geq 1
and
there exists

\rho\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(m^{\prime},e)}{s}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}
with
m>m^{\prime}
.

2.

Exponential
:
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}s
then
e\geq 1
and
there exists

\rho\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(m,e^{\prime})}{s}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}
with
e>e^{\prime}
.

3.

GC by value
:
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}s
then
there exists

\rho\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(m,e)}{s}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}
with
|\pi|>|\rho|
.

Note that
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
steps do not change the
m
and
e
indices. This is a consequence of rules
@
and
\mathsf{ES}
having been modified for CbS with
\uplus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}
(and not with
\uplus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}
as in
[
42
]
).

Theorem 11

(Weak SSC correctness)
.

Let
t
be a term.
If
\pi\,\triangleright{}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(m,e)}{t}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}
then
t\in\mbox{SN}_{\mathtt{w}}
. Moreover, if
d:t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}^{*}n
is a normalizing sequence then
|d|_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}\leq m
and
|d|_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}\leq e
.

Proof.

By lexicographic induction on
(m+e,|\pi|)
and case analysis on whether
t
reduces or not. If
t
is
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
-normal then the statement trivially holds. If
t
is not
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
-normal we show that all its reducts are SN for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
, that is,
t
is SN. If
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}u
then by quantitative subject reduction (Prop.
28
) there is a derivation
\rho\,\triangleright{}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(m^{\prime},e)}{u}\ {:}\ {A}
with
m^{\prime}<m
. By
i.h.
,
u
is SN. If
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}u
or
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}u
we reason similarly, looking at the
e
index for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
and to the size
|\rho|
of the derivation for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
.
The
moreover
part follows from the fact that
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}
(resp.
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
) steps strictly decrease
m
(resp.
e
).
∎

Completeness.

For completeness, we first need typability of weak normal forms. The two points of the next proposition are proved by mutual induction. The second point is stronger, as it has a universal quantification about linear types, crucial for the induction to go through.

{toappendix}

Proposition 12

(Weak normal forms are typable)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
C

1.

Let
a
be a weak answer. Then there exists
\pi\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(0,0)}{a}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}}
with
\mathtt{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}})=\mathtt{shfv}(a)
.

2.

Let
i
be an inert term. Then for any linear type
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}
there exist a type context
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}
such that
\mathtt{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}})=\mathtt{shfv}(i)
and a derivation
\pi\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(0,0)}{i}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}
.

The rest of the proof of completeness is dual to the one for correctness, with an anti-substitution lemma (in the Appendix) needed for subject expansion for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
. We omit the indices because for completeness they are irrelevant.

{toappendix}

Proposition 13

(Subject expansion for Weak SSC)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
C

Let
\pi\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}{s}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}
be a derivation.
If
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}s
then
there exists a derivation

\rho\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}{t}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}
.

Theorem 14

(Weak SSC completeness)
.

Let
t
be a term.
If
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}^{*}n
and
n
is a weak normal form then there exists
\pi\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}{t}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}}
.

Proof.

By induction on
k=|d|
. If
k=0
: then
t=n
and is typable with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}
by Prop.
12
. If
k>0
then
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}u\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}^{k-1}n
for some
u
and by
i.h.
there is a derivation
\pi^{\prime}\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}{u}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}
.
By subject expansion
(Prop.
13
),
\pi\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}{t}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}
.
∎

Corollary 15

(Weak SSC Uniform normalization)
.

t
is weakly
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
-normalizing if and only if
t
is strongly
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
-normalizing.

Proof.

The non-obvious direction is
\Rightarrow
. By completeness (Th.
14
),
t\in\mbox{WN}_{\mathtt{w}}
implies typability, which in turn, by correctness (Th.
11
), implies
t\in\mbox{SN}_{\mathtt{w}}
.
∎

7
Call-by-Value and Operational Equivalence

Here, we show that the silly multi types characterize CbV termination as well, and infer the operational equivalence of the SSC and CbV.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{.25cm}}rcl}\textsc{Terms}\hskip 7.11317pt&t,s,u&::=&x\mid\lambda x.t\mid ts\\
\textsc{Values}\hskip 7.11317pt&v,v^{\prime}&::=&\lambda x.t\\[6.0pt]
\textsc{{CbV} contexts}\hskip 7.11317pt&V&::=&\langle\cdot\rangle\mid tV\mid Vt\end{array}

Rewriting rule

\begin{array}[]{rll}(\lambda x.t)v&\mapsto_{\beta_{v}}&t\{x{\shortleftarrow}v\}\\[6.0pt]
\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}&:=&V\langle\mapsto_{\beta_{v}}\rangle\end{array}

Figure 3:
The call-by-value
\lambda
-calculus.

Closed CbV without ESs.

We define the CbV
\lambda
-calculus in Fig.
3
, mostly following the presentation of Dal Lago and Martini
[
29
]
, for which the
\beta_{v}
-rule is non-deterministic but diamond (thus trivially uniformly normalizing, see the rewriting preliminaries). The only change with respect to
[
29
]
is that here values are only abstractions, for uniformity with the Weak SSC. We shall consider the weak evaluation of closed terms only, for which there is no difference whether variables are values or not, since free variables cannot be arguments (out of abstractions) anyway. Closed normal forms are exactly the abstractions.

We discuss only the closed case for CbV because the silly type system is
not
correct for CbV with open terms. This point is properly explained after the operational equivalence theorem. It is also the reason why we do not present CbV via the LSC, as also explained after the theorem. The problem with open terms does not hinder the operational equivalence of the SSC and CbV, because contextual equivalence is based on closed terms only.

Judgements for CbV.

In this section, the index
e
of the silly type system does not play any role, because
\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}
steps are bound only by the
m
index. Therefore, we omit
e
and write
\pi\,\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}{}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{m}{t}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}
instead of
\pi\,\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}{}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(m,e)}{t}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}
.

Correctness.

The proof technique is the standard one. As usual, subject reduction is proved via a substitution lemma in the Appendix. The index
m
is used as decreasing measure to prove correctness.

{toappendix}

Proposition 16

(Quantitative subject reduction for Closed CbV)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
D

Let
t
be a closed term. If
\pi\,\triangleright{}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{m}{t}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}

and
t\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}s

then
m\geq 1
and
there exists

\rho\,\triangleright{}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{m^{\prime}}{s}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}

with
m>m^{\prime}
.

{toappendix}

Theorem 17

(Closed CbV correctness)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
D

Let
t
be a closed term.
If
\pi\,\triangleright{}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{m}{t}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}
then there are an abstraction
v
and a reduction sequence
d\colon t\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}^{*}v
with
|d|\leq m
.

Completeness is also proved in a standard way, omitting the index
m
because it is irrelevant.

{toappendix}

Proposition 18

(Subject expansion for Closed CbV)
.
†
†
margin:
