If

\pi\,\triangleright{}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}{s}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}

and
t\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}s

then and
there exists a typing
\rho
such that
\rho\,\triangleright{}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}{t}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{L}}}
.

{toappendix}

Theorem 19

(Closed CbV completeness)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
D

Let
t
be a closed
\lambda
-term.
If there exists a value
v
and a reduction sequence
d\colon t\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}^{*}v
then
\pi\,\triangleright{}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}{t}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}}
.

Operational Equivalence.

We define abstractly contextual equivalence for a language of terms and arbitrary contexts which are terms with an additional hole construct.

Definition 20

(Contextual Equivalence)
.

Given a rewriting relation
\rightarrow
, we define the associated
contextual equivalence

\simeq_{\mathtt{C}}
as follows:

t\simeq_{\mathtt{C}}t^{\prime}
if, for all contexts
C
such that
C\langle t\rangle
and
C\langle t^{\prime}\rangle
are closed terms,

C\langle t\rangle
is weakly
\rightarrow
-normalizing iff
C\langle t^{\prime}\rangle
is weakly
\rightarrow
-normalizing.

Let
\simeq_{C}^{\mathtt{silly}}
and
\simeq_{C}^{\mathtt{value}}
be the contextual equivalences for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
and
\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}
. The next section shall show that
\simeq_{C}^{\mathtt{silly}}
can equivalently be defined using the CbS strategy.

Theorem 21

(Operational equivalence of CbS and CbV)
.

On
\lambda
-terms,
t\simeq_{C}^{\mathtt{silly}}s
iff
t\simeq_{C}^{\mathtt{value}}s
.

Proof.

On closed
\lambda
-terms, both
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
-termination and
\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}
-termination are equivalent to typability in the silly system (Theorems
11
and
14
for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}}
and Theorems
17
and
19
for
\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}
). Thus the contextual equivalences coincide.
∎

Call-by-Silly Helps to Prove Contextual Equivalence.

As the last theorem says, contextual equivalences induced by CbV and CbS coincide. Even though they equate the same terms, CbS reduction sometimes provides a way to prove CbV contextual equivalence in cases where CbV does not. Consider the following four different terms, where
i
could be any normal form that is not of the shape
S\langle v\rangle
, for example
i=y\mathtt{I}
:

(\lambda x.xx)\,i

(\lambda x.xi)\,i

(\lambda x.ii)\,i

ii

These four terms can intuitively be seen as CbV contextually equivalent, as we now outline. When one of these terms is plugged in a closing context
C
, reduction shall provide substitutions on
i
making it either converge to a value
v
or diverge. if it diverges, so will the four terms. If it converges to
v
, then all four terms will reduce to
vv
. This reasoning however cannot easily be made formal.

The easiest way to prove that two terms are contextually equivalent for a reduction
\rightarrow_{r}
is to prove that they are related by
=_{r}
, the smallest equivalence relation including
\rightarrow_{r}
. We shall now see how the silly calculus helps in equating more terms (than CbV) with its reduction.

The four terms above are not
=_{\beta_{v}}
-related.
First, note that the four terms are all
\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}
-normal (assuming that
i
is
\rightarrow_{\beta_{v}}
-normal). If they were
=_{\beta_{v}}
-related, then by the Church-Rosser property they should have a common reduct. As the four terms are syntactically different normal forms, they cannot be equated by
=_{\beta_{v}}
.

The first three terms are
=_{\mathtt{w}}
-related.
The first three terms rewrite in the SSC to
ii[x{\shortleftarrow}i]
, which is why
(\lambda x.xx)\,i=_{\mathtt{w}}(\lambda x.xi)\,i=_{\mathtt{w}}(\lambda x.ii)\,i=_{\mathtt{w}}ii[x{\shortleftarrow}i]
.

Unfortunately, the fourth term
ii
is a
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
-normal form and hence
ii\neq_{\mathtt{w}}ii[x{\shortleftarrow}i]
. Thus, not all CbV contextually equivalent terms are equated by the silly calculus, not even when the difference amounts to the duplication of a non-value term. The issue has to do with the
silly extra copy
that cannot be erased easily. We believe that a small-step silly calculus could help equating more terms, but we have not managed to work out multi types for such a calculus.

We refer the reader to the Appendix (Th.
44
) for the proof that
=_{\mathtt{w}}
-related terms are contextually equivalent.

The Issue with Open CbV and the Silly Type System.

There is an issue if one considers the silly type system relatively to CbV with open terms, namely subject reduction breaks. This point is delicate. In fact, there are no issues if one considers only Plotkin’s
\beta_{v}
rule, except that Plotkin’s rule is
not
an adequate operational semantics for CbV with open terms, as it is well-known and discussed at length by Accattoli and Guerrieri
[
10
,
11
]
. Adequate operational semantics for Open CbV do extend Plotkin’s. One such semantics is Carraro and Guerrieri’s
shuffling calculus

[
27
]
, that extends
\beta_{v}
by adding some
\sigma
-rules. We briefly discuss it here; the last paragraph of this section shall explain because we prefer it to the CbV LSC for the explanation of the issue with open terms.

It turns out that one of the
\sigma
rules breaks subject reduction for the silly type system (while there are no problems if one considers instead Ehrhard’s CbV multi types
[
32
]
), as we now show. Rule
\mapsto_{\sigma_{3}}
is defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}z((\lambda x.y)s)&\mapsto_{\sigma_{3}}&(\lambda x.zy)s\end{array}

For
n\geq 1
, we have the following derivation for the source term
z((\lambda x.y)s)
in the silly type system:

\pi_{z}\,\triangleright\ldots

\mathsf{ax}

y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}\vdash y\,{:}\,A

\lambda

y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}\vdash\lambda x.y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbf{0}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}A

\pi_{s}\,\triangleright{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash s\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}

\mathsf{many}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash s\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}

@

(y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash(\lambda x.y)s\,{:}\,A)_{i=1,\ldots,n}

\mathsf{ax}

y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}\vdash y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}

\lambda

y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}\vdash\lambda x.y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbf{0}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}

\pi_{s}\,\triangleright{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash s\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}

\mathsf{many}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash s\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}

@

y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash(\lambda x.y)s\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}

\mathsf{many}

y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}^{n+1}\vdash(\lambda x.y)s\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}

@

z\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}B{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}},y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}^{n+1}\vdash z((\lambda x.y)s)\,{:}\,B

where
\pi_{z}\,\triangleright\ldots
stands for:

\mathsf{ax}

\pi_{z}\,\triangleright z\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}B{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}\vdash z\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}B

The target term
(\lambda x.zy)s
of rule
\mapsto_{\sigma_{3}}
, instead, can only be typed as follows, the key point being that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}^{n+1}
is replaced by
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}
:

\mathsf{ax}

z\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}B{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}\vdash z\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}B

\mathsf{ax}

(y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}\vdash y\,{:}\,A)_{i=1,\ldots,n}

\mathsf{ax}

y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}\vdash y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}

\mathsf{many}

y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}\vdash y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}

@

z\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}B{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}},y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}\vdash zy\,{:}\,B

\lambda

z\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}B{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}},y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}\vdash\lambda x.zy\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbf{0}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}B

\pi_{s}\,\triangleright{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash s\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}

\mathsf{many}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash s\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}

@

z\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}B{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}},y\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash(\lambda x.zy)s\,{:}\,B

This counter-example adapt the counter-example given by Delia Kesner to subject reduction for the multi type system by Manzonetto et al.
[
46
,
37
]
, as reported in the long version on Arxiv of
[
11
]
, which appeared after the publication of
[
46
,
37
]
, where there is no mention of this issue. The work in the present paper can be actually seen as a clarification of the failure of subject reduction for the system in
[
46
,
37
]
. Essentially, the system in
[
46
,
37
]
is a system for CbS, not for CbV, but is therein used to study CbV strong evaluation with possibly open terms, unaware that the system models a different evaluation mechanism.

We conjecture that the silly type system is adequate for Open CbV (that is, a term is silly typable if and only if it is CbV terminating) even if it is not invariant for Open CbV (that is, subject reduction does not hold).

Naturality of the Issue.

A first reaction to the shown issue is to suspect that something is wrong or ad-hoc in our approach, especially given that the operational equivalence of CbN and CbNeed does not suffer of this issue, that is, the CbN multi type system is invariant for CbNeed evaluation of open terms. At high-level, however, the issue is natural and to be expected, as we now explain. The two systems of each pair CbN/CbNeed and CbS/CbV have different duplicating policies and the same erasing policy. The pair CbN/CbNeed has no restrictions on erasure, thus open normal forms have no garbage. Therefore, the different ways in which they duplicate garbage are not observable. For the pair CbS/CbV, instead, erasure is restricted to
values
, with the consequence that garbage has to be evaluated before possibly being erased, and that with open terms some garbage might never be erased. Thus, the different duplicating policies of CbS/CbV leave different amounts of non-erasable garbage in open normal forms, which is observable and changes the denotational semantics.

The Open CbV LSC.

Another adequate formalism for Open CbV is the VSC, or its micro-step variant, the CbV LSC. We now discuss the CbV LSC, but everything we say applies also to the VSC.

The difference between the shuffling calculus and the CbV LSC is that the latter uses ESs and modifies the rewriting rules at a distance. In particular, duplication is done by the following exponential rule:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}W\langle x\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}S\langle v\rangle]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}\mathtt{e}}&S\langle W\langle v\rangle[x{\shortleftarrow}v]\rangle\end{array}

Subject reduction breaks also for the CbV LSC, as we can show by adapting Kesner’s counter-example. Consider the step
(zy^{\prime})[y^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}[y{\shortleftarrow}s]]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}\mathtt{e}}(z\mathtt{I})[y^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}s]
:

\pi_{1}\,\triangleright z\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}B{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}},y^{\prime}\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}\vdash zy^{\prime}\,{:}\,B

\pi_{\mathtt{I}}\vdash\mathtt{I}\,{:}\,A

\pi_{s}\,\triangleright{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash s\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}

\mathsf{many}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash s\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}

\mathsf{ES}

({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash\mathtt{I}[y{\shortleftarrow}s]\,{:}\,A)_{i=1,\ldots,n}

\mathsf{ax}_{\lambda}

\vdash\mathtt{I}\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}

\pi_{s}\,\triangleright{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash s\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}

\mathsf{many}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash s\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}

\mathsf{ES}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash\mathtt{I}[y{\shortleftarrow}s]\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}

\mathsf{many}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}^{n+1}\vdash\mathtt{I}[y{\shortleftarrow}s]\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}

\mathsf{ES}

z\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}B{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}^{n+1}\vdash(zy^{\prime})[y^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}[y{\shortleftarrow}s]]\,{:}\,B

As for
\mapsto_{\sigma_{3}}
, the key point is that that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}^{n+1}
gets replaced by
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}
in the typing of the reduct:

\mathsf{ax}

z\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}B{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}\vdash z\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}B

(\pi_{\mathtt{I}}\vdash\mathtt{I}\,{:}\,A)_{i=1,\ldots,n}

\mathsf{ax}_{\lambda}

\vdash\mathtt{I}\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}

\mathsf{many}

\vdash\mathtt{I}\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}

\mathsf{ES}

z\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}B{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}\vdash z\mathtt{I}\,{:}\,B

\mathsf{ax}_{\lambda}

\vdash\mathtt{I}\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}

\mathsf{many}

\vdash\mathtt{I}\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}

\mathsf{ES}

z\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}B{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}\vdash(z\mathtt{I})[y^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]\,{:}\,B

\pi_{s}\,\triangleright{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash s\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}

\mathsf{many}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash s\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}

\mathsf{ES}

z\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}A^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}B{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}}\vdash(z\mathtt{I})[y^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}s]\,{:}\,B

What breaks it is the use of the substitution context
S
in
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}\mathtt{e}}
, which can be seen as the analogous of rule
\mapsto_{\sigma_{3}}
of the shuffling calculus. The difference is that while
\mapsto_{\sigma_{3}}
is needed only for open terms in the shuffling calculus, rule
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}\mathtt{e}}
is used also for the evaluation of closed terms in the CbV LSC. This is why we preferred to avoid using the CbV LSC to study Closed CbV. In fact, one can prove that in the closed case the modification of rule
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{v}\mathtt{e}}
without
S
is enough to reach normal forms. But this fact needs a technical study and a theorem, which we preferred to avoid.

8
The Call-by-Silly Strategy

In this section, we define the CbS evaluation strategy as a sort of extension of the CbN one. We then start by recalling the CbN erasing rule and the CbN strategy.

​​​​​​​​​​

\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}\lx@intercol\hfil\textsc{Call-by-name strategy }\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[4.0pt]
\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{.15cm}}r@{\hspace{.1cm}}l@{\hspace{.1cm}}ll}\textsc{Name ctxs}\hskip 4.26773pt&N,N^{\prime}\hskip 2.84544pt&::=\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\langle\cdot\rangle\mid Nt\mid N[x{\shortleftarrow}t]\\[5.0pt]
\textsc{Root GC}\hskip 4.26773pt&t[x{\shortleftarrow}s]\hskip 2.84544pt&\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}}\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&t\ \ \ \mbox{if }x\notin\mathtt{fv}(t)\end{array}&&&\begin{array}[]{rcl}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{m}}&:=&N\langle\mapsto_{\mathtt{m}}\rangle\\
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{e}}&:=&N\langle\mapsto_{\mathtt{e}_{N}}\rangle\\
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{gc}}&:=&N\langle\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}}\rangle\\
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}&:=&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{m}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{e}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}\end{array}\end{array}

​​​​​​​​​​

\begin{array}[]{cc|cc}\lx@intercol\hfil\textsc{Call-by-silly strategy }\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[4.0pt]
\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{.25cm}}r@{\hspace{.1cm}}l@{\hspace{.1cm}}ll}\textsc{Answers}\hskip 7.11317pt&a,a^{\prime}\hskip 2.84544pt&::=\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&v\mid a[x{\shortleftarrow}a^{\prime}]\\[3.0pt]
\textsc{Aux. ctxs}\hskip 7.11317pt&A,A^{\prime}\hskip 2.84544pt&::=\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&\langle\cdot\rangle\mid a[x{\shortleftarrow}A]\mid A[x{\shortleftarrow}t]\\[3.0pt]
\textsc{Silly ctxs}\hskip 7.11317pt&Y,Y^{\prime}\hskip 2.84544pt&::=\hfil\hskip 2.84544pt&A\langle N\rangle\end{array}&&&\begin{array}[]{rll}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{m}}}&:=&Y\langle\mapsto_{\mathtt{m}}\rangle\\
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}&:=&Y\langle\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}\rangle\\
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{AY}}}&:=&A\langle\mapsto_{\mathtt{e}_{Y}}\rangle\\
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{YN}}}&:=&Y\langle\mapsto_{\mathtt{e}_{N}}\rangle\\
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}}&:=&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{m}}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{AY}}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{YN}}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 4:
The call-by-name and call-by-silly strategies.

CbN Erasure.

The
garbage collection (GC) rule

\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}}
in Fig.
4
eliminates the ES
t[x{\shortleftarrow}s]
when the bound variable
x
does not occur in
t
. Rule
\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}}
is the CbN form of erasure and it is not part of the rules of the SSC; it is given here only to be able to define the CbN strategy. Example:
(\lambda z.yy)[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}[w{\shortleftarrow}t]]\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}}\lambda z.yy
.

Call-by-Name.

In Fig.
4
, we define the CbN strategy
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}
of the weak LSC, first appeared in Accattoli et al.
[
5
]
. The CbN strategy uses the CbN GC rule
\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}}
. Note that CbN evaluation contexts
N
never enter into arguments or ESs. The CbN strategy is almost deterministic: rules
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{m}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{e}}
are deterministic and its erasing rule
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{gc}}
is non-deterministic but diamond; for instance
\mathtt{I}[z{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]\prescript{}{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{gc}}{\leftarrow}\mathtt{I}[y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][z{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{gc}}\mathtt{I}[y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]
, and then
\mathtt{I}[z{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{gc}}\mathtt{I}\prescript{}{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{gc}}{\leftarrow}\mathtt{I}[y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]
. Moreover,
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{gc}}
is postponable, i.e. if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}}^{*}s
then
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\neg\mathtt{gc}}^{*}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{gc}}^{*}s
where
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\neg\mathtt{gc}}:=\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{m}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{e}}
, which is why it is often omitted from the micro-step presentation of CbN.

The Call-by-Silly Strategy.

The CbS strategy
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}}
is defined in Fig.
4
via silly evaluation contexts
Y
—explained next—(we use
Y
for
sillY
because
S
is already used for substitution contexts) and the CbV erasing rule
\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
of the SSC. Letting GC aside, CbS is the extension of CbN that, once (non-erasing) CbN can no longer reduce, starts evaluating the ESs (out of abstractions) left hanging by the CbN strategy. Such an extension is specified via the
auxiliary contexts

A
, whose key production is
t[x{\shortleftarrow}A]
for evaluation contexts where
t
is a CbS normal form: evaluation enters an ESs only when it is sure that it is no longer needed. There are, however, a few subtleties.

Firstly,
no longer needed
does not necessarily mean
without free occurrences
, as occurrences might be blocked by abstractions, as for instance in
(\lambda y.x)[x{\shortleftarrow}s]
where
x
occurs but only under abstraction, and the CbS has to evaluate inside
s
. Therefore,
no longer needed
rather means
without shallow free occurrences
(Def.
1
, page
1
).

Secondly, in order to be sure that no occurrences of
x
shall become shallow because of other steps, we shall ask that in
t[x{\shortleftarrow}Y]
the sub-term
t
is a normal form, otherwise in a case such as
((\lambda y.x)\mathtt{I})[x{\shortleftarrow}s]
the reduction of the multiplicative step to
x[y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][x{\shortleftarrow}s]
turns a blocked occurrence of
x
into a shallow occurrence. Because of rules at a distance, the term
(\lambda y.x)[x{\shortleftarrow}s]\mathtt{I}
suffers of the same problem but the argument comes
on the right
of the ES. For this reason, we forbid the evaluation context to be applied once it enters an ES, that is, silly contexts
Y
are defined as
A\langle N\rangle
, that is, rigidly separating the construction that applies (at work in
N
) from the one that enters ES (at work in
A
).

Thirdly, such a rigid separation in the definition of silly contexts forces us to have
two
exponential rules. Exponential rules use contexts twice in their definition: to select the occurrence to replace in the root rule
and
to extend the applicability of the root rule. The subtlety is that if both these contexts are silly, then one can nest an
A
context (selecting an occurrence) under a
N
context (extending the rule), which, as explained, has to be avoided. Therefore there are two silly exponential rules
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{AY}}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{YN}}}
carefully designed as to avoid the dangerous combination (that would be given by
N\langle\mapsto_{\mathtt{e}_{A}}\rangle
).

Fourthly, in order to mimic the property of CbN that GC is postponable, we shall ask that in
t[x{\shortleftarrow}Y]
the sub-term
t
is a normal form only for the
non-erasing
CbS, otherwise in a case such as
x[y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][x{\shortleftarrow}s]
the CbS strategy would be forced to erase
[y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]
before evaluating
s
. Normal forms for non-erasing CbS are characterized below (Prop.
23
) exactly as the answers defined in Fig.
4
. We shall also prove that
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}
steps are postponable (Prop.
3
).

The CbS strategy is supposed to be applied to closed terms only, while there is no closure hypothesis on the weak calculus.

Example 22
.

A good example to observe the differences between CbN and CbS is given by the term
t:=(\lambda y.\lambda x.x(\lambda w.x))\Omega(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})
where
\mathtt{I}:=\lambda z.z
,
\Omega:=\delta\delta
, and
\delta:=\lambda z.zz
. In CbN, it evaluates with 4 multiplicative steps and 3 exponential steps, as follows:

\begin{array}[]{llll}t&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{m}}(\lambda x.x(\lambda w.x))[y{\shortleftarrow}\Omega](\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})&&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{m}}(x(\lambda w.x))[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\Omega]\\
&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{e}}((\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})(\lambda w.x))[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\Omega]&&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{m}}(z[z{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}](\lambda w.x))[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\Omega]\\
&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{e}}(\mathtt{I}[z{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}](\lambda w.x))[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\Omega]&&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{m}}z^{\prime}[z^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\lambda w.x][z{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\Omega]\\
&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{e}}(\lambda w.x)[z^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\lambda w.x][z{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\Omega]&&=:s\end{array}

The obtained term
s
is normal for the multiplicative and exponential rules. Since in CbN one can erase every term,
s
then reduces (in CbN) as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lllllllll}s&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{gc}}&(\lambda w.x)[z{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\Omega]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{gc}}&(\lambda w.x)[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\Omega]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{gc}}&(\lambda w.x)[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]\end{array}

The obtained term is now normal for the CbN strategy, since evaluation does not enter into ESs.
Note that
t
diverges for CbV evaluation (defined in Sect.
7
). In CbS, the first part of the evaluation of
t
, up to
s
, is the same as for CbN (where
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{n}\mathtt{e}}
steps become
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{YN}}}
steps). Then,
s
evaluates differently than in CbN, diverging:

\begin{array}[]{lllllllll}s&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}&(\lambda w.x)[z{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\Omega]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}&(\lambda w.x)[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\Omega]\\
&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{m}}}&(\lambda w.x)[x{\shortleftarrow}z[z{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]][y{\shortleftarrow}\Omega]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{AY}}}&(\lambda w.x)[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}[z{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]][y{\shortleftarrow}\Omega]\\
&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}&(\lambda w.x)[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\Omega]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{m}}}&(\lambda w.x)[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}zz[z{\shortleftarrow}\delta]]&...\end{array}

Rewriting Properties of CbS.

We start by characterizing normal form for both the CbS strategy and its non-erasing variant
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}:=\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{m}}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{AY}}}\cup\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{YN}}}
.

{toappendix}

Proposition 23
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
E.1

Let
t
be closed.

1.

t
is
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
-normal if and only if
t
is
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}
-normal if and only if
t
is an answer.

2.

t
is
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
-normal if and only if
t
is
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}}
-normal if and only if
t
is a strict answer:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{.5cm}} rll}\textsc{Strict answers}\hskip 14.22636pt&a_{s}&::=&v\mid a_{s}[x{\shortleftarrow}a_{s}^{\prime}]\ \mbox{ with }x\in\mathtt{fv}(a_{s})\end{array}

Next, we prove that the CbS strategy is almost deterministic. Its non-erasing rules are deterministic, while
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}
is diamond. For instance:

\mathtt{I}[z{\shortleftarrow}\delta]\prescript{}{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}{\leftarrow}\mathtt{I}[y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][z{\shortleftarrow}\delta]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}\mathtt{I}[y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]
, and then
\mathtt{I}[z{\shortleftarrow}\delta]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}\mathtt{I}\prescript{}{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}{\leftarrow}\mathtt{I}[y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]
.

{toappendix}

Proposition 24
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
E.2

Reductions
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{m}}}
,
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{AY}}}
, and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{YN}}}
are deterministic, and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}
is diamond.

Lastly, we prove the
linear
postponement of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}
, that is, the fact that it can be postponed while preserving the length of the evaluation. The length preservation shall be used to prove the maximality of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}}
in the next section.

{toappendix}

Proposition 25

(Postponement of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}
)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
E.3

If
d:t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}}^{*}s
then
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}^{k}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}^{h}s
with
k=|d|_{\mathtt{y}\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
and
h=|d|_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
.

9
Tight Derivations, Exact Lengths, and Maximality

Here, we focus on the CbS strategy on closed terms and isolate a class of
tight
type derivations whose indices measure exactly the number of non-erasing CbS steps, mimicking faithfully what was done in call-by-name/value/need by Accattoli et al. in
[
12
]
. An outcome shall be that the CbS strategy actually performs the
longest
weak evaluation on closed terms.

Tight Derivations.

The basic tool for our quantitative analysis are tight type derivations, which shall be used to refine the correctness theorem of the weak case (Th.
11
) in the case of closed terms. Tight derivations are defined via a predicate on their last judgement, as in
[
12
]
.

Definition 26

(Tight types and derivations)
.

A type
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{T}}
is
tight
if
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{T}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}
.
A derivation
\pi\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(m,e)}{t\!}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{T}}}
is
tight
if
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{T}}
is tight.

Tight Correctness.

We first show that all tight derivations for answers have null indices.

{toappendix}

Proposition 27

(Tight typing of normal forms for non-erasing CbS)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
F

Let
a
be an answer and

\pi\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(m,e)}{a}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}}
be a derivation.
Then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}
is empty and
m=e=0
.

Next, we refine subject reduction via tight derivations. The key point is that now the indices decrease of
exactly one
at each step. Tight correctness then follows.

{toappendix}

Proposition 28

(Tight subject reduction for non-erasing CbS)
.
†
†
margin:

Proof at p.
F

Let
\pi\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(m,e)}{t}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}}
be a tight derivation.

1.

Multiplicative
:
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{m}}}s
then
m\geq 1
and
there is

\rho\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(m-1,e)}{s}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}}
.

2.

Exponential
:
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{AY}}}s
or
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{YN}}}s
then
e\geq 1
and
there is

\rho\,\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(m,e-1)}{s}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}}
.

Theorem 29

(Tight correctness for CbS)
.

Let
t
be a closed term and
\pi\,\triangleright{}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(m,e)}{t}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}}
be a tight derivation.
Then there is a weak normal form
n
such that
d\colon t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}}^{*}n
with
|d|_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{m}}=m
and
|d|_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{AY}},\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{YN}}}=e
.

Proof.

The proof is exactly as for weak correctness (Th.
11
), except that if
t
is normal then the fact that
m=e=0
follows from Prop.
27
and that if
t
is not normal the equality on the number of steps is obtained by using tight subject reduction (Prop.
28
) instead of the quantitative one.
∎

For tight completeness for closed terms, it is enough to observe that the statement of weak completeness (Th.
14
) already gives a tight derivation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}\vdash t\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}
, the type context
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\Gamma}}
of which is empty because
t
is closed (Lemma
9
).

Example 30
.

We illustrate the tightness of multi types for CbS with an example. Consider the term
(\lambda y.yy)(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})
, the CbS evaluation of which was given in Example
22
(page
22
) and is as follows (the first part coincides with the CbN evaluation):

\begin{array}[]{rll}\lx@intercol\textsc{CbN evaluation:}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
(\lambda y.yy)(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})\\
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{m}}}&yy[y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{YN}}}(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})y[y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]\\
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{m}}}&(x[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}])y[y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{YN}}}(\mathtt{I}[x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}])y[y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]\par\\
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{m}}}&z[z{\shortleftarrow}y][x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{YN}}}y[z{\shortleftarrow}y][x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]\par\\
&&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{YN}}}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}[z{\shortleftarrow}y][x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]\par\\
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{m}}}&z^{\prime}[z^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][z{\shortleftarrow}y][x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{YN}}}\mathtt{I}[z^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][z{\shortleftarrow}y][x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]\par\\[5.0pt]
\hline\cr\\[-5.0pt]
\lx@intercol\textsc{CbS extension:}\hfil\lx@intercol\par\\
&&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{AY}}}\mathtt{I}[z^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][z{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}][x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]\par\\
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{m}}}&\mathtt{I}[z^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][z{\shortleftarrow}z^{\prime}[z^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]][x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{YN}}}\mathtt{I}[z^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][z{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}[z^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]][x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]\par\\
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{m}}}&\mathtt{I}[z^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][z{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}[z^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]][x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}z^{\prime}[z^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]]&\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{YN}}}\mathtt{I}[z^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][z{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}[z^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]][x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}[z^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}]]\end{array}

If we consider CbN evaluation, evaluation stops earlier: it would reach a normal form with
\mathtt{I}[z^{\prime}{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][z{\shortleftarrow}y][x{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}][y{\shortleftarrow}\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}]
, that is after
4
multiplicative and
5
exponential steps.
CbS evaluates more than CbN, hence the rewriting sequence ends only after
6
multiplicative and
8
exponential steps.

We now show a tight derivation for the term
(\lambda y.yy)(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})
, which is indeed indexed by
(6,8)
, as per tight correctness and completeness (Th.
29
and the text after it). For compactness, we shorten
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}
as
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}}
. Moreover, to fit the derivation in the page, we first give the main derivation, giving a name to some sub-derivations which are shown after the main one.

•

Main derivarion:

\pi{}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(1,3)}{\lambda y.yy}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}}}

\rho{}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(1,2)}{\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}}}

(\rho_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}}{}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(1,1)}{\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}}})\times 3

{}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(4,5)}{\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I}}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}\uplus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{[}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{]}}}

{}\ {\boldsymbol{\vdash}}^{(6,8)}{(\lambda y.yy)(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})}\ {:}\ {{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{n}}}}

•

Maximality of the CbS Strategy.

Similarly to how we proved uniform normalization for
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
, we can prove that on closed terms the CbS strategy does reach a weak normal form whenever one exists—the key point being that
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}}
does not stop too soon. This fact proves that
\simeq_{C}^{\mathtt{silly}}
can equivalently be defined using
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}}
, as mentioned in Sect.
7
. Moreover, by exploiting tight correctness and some of the rewriting properties of Sect.
4
, we prove that the CbS strategy is maximal.

Proposition 31
.

Let
t
be closed and
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}^{h}a_{s}
with
a_{s}
a strict answer.

1.

CbS is normalizing
:
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}}^{k}a_{s}
for some
k\in{\rm Nature}
;

2.

CbS is maximal
:
h\leq k
.

Proof.

1.

By completeness (Th.
14
),
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}^{h}a_{s}
implies typability of
t
(a strict answer is in particular a weak normal form), which in turn, by tight correctness (Th.
29
), implies
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}}^{k}a_{s}^{\prime}
for some
k\in{\rm Nature}
. By confluence (Th.
7
),
a_{s}=a_{s}^{\prime}
.

2.

By postponement of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
(Prop.
3
) applied to
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}^{h}a_{s}
, we obtain a sequence
d:t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}^{h_{1}}s\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}^{h_{2}}a_{s}
with
h_{1}+h_{2}\geq h
, for some
s
. For the strategy sequence
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}}^{k}a_{s}
given by Point 1, the
moreover
part of the postponement property gives us a sequence
e:t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}^{k_{1}}u\rightarrow_{\mathtt{y}{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}}^{k_{2}}a_{s}
with, crucially,
k_{1}+k_{2}=k
, for some
u
.
By completeness of the type system (Th.
14
), we obtain a derivation
\vdash^{(m,e)}t\,{:}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathtt{norm}}}
.
By weak correctness (Th.
11
), we obtain
h_{1}\leq m+e
. By tight correctness (Th.
29
),
k_{1}=m+e
. Thus,
h_{1}\leq k_{1}
. Now, note that both
s
and
u
are
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
-normal, because otherwise, by (iterated) strict commutation of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
(Lemma
6
), we obtain that
a_{s}
is not
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
-normal, against hypothesis. Since
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
is confluent (Th.
7
),
s=u
. Since
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
is diamond (Lemma
5
),
h_{2}=k_{2}
. Then
k=k_{1}+k_{2}=k_{1}+h_{2}\geq h_{1}+h_{2}=h
. ∎

Why not the
\lambda
-Calculus?

In the
\lambda
-calculus, it is hard to specify via evaluation contexts the idea behind the CbS strategy of
evaluating arguments only when they are no longer needed
. The difficulty is specific to weak evaluation. For instance, for
t:=(\lambda y.\lambda x.yy)u
the CbS strategy should evaluate
u
before substituting it for
y
, because once
u
ends up under
\lambda x
it shall be unreachable by weak evaluation. For
s:=(\lambda y.(\lambda x.yy)r)u
, instead, the CbS strategy should not evaluate
u
before substituting it, because weak evaluation will reach the two occurrences of
y
, and one obtains a longer reduction by substituting
u
before evaluating it. Note that one cannot decide what to do with
u
in
t
and
s
by checking if
y
occurs inside the abstraction, because
y
occurs under
\lambda y
in both
t
and
s
.

Multi types naturally make the right choices for
t
(evaluating
u
before substituting it) and
s
(substituting
u
before evaluating it), so a strategy matching exactly the bounds given by multi types needs to do the same choices.
The LSC allows one to bypass the difficulty, by first turning
\beta
-redexes into explicit substitutions, thus exposing
y
out of abstractions in
s
but not in
t
, and matching what is measured by multi types. In the
\lambda
-calculus, we have not found natural ways of capturing this aspect (be careful: the example illustrates the problem but solving the problem requires more than just handling the example).

10
Conclusions

We introduce the weak silly substitution calculus and the CbS strategy by mirroring the properties of CbNeed with respect to duplication and erasure. Then, we provide evidence of the good design of the framework via a rewriting study of the calculus and the strategy, and by mirroring the semantic analyses of CbNeed via multi types by Kesner
[
38
]
(qualitative) and Accattoli et al.
[
12
]
(quantitative).

Conceptually, the main results are the operational equivalence of CbS and CbV, mirroring the one between CbN and CbNeed, and the exact measuring of CbS evaluation lengths via multi types, having the interesting corollary that CbS is
maximal
in the Weak SSC.
It would be interesting to show that, dually, the CbNeed strategy computes the shortest reduction in the CbNeed LSC. We are not aware of any such result.

Our work also shows that CbV contextual equivalence
\simeq_{C}^{\mathtt{value}}
is completely blind to the efficiency of evaluation. We think that it is important to look for natural refinements of
\simeq_{C}^{\mathtt{value}}
which are less blind, or, in the opposite direction, exploring what are the minimal extensions of CbV that make
\simeq_{C}^{\mathtt{value}}
efficiency-sensitive.

We would also like to develop categorical and game semantics for both CbS and CbNeed, to pinpoint the principles behind the wiseness and the silliness of duplications and erasures.

Appendix A
Proofs of Sect.
4
(Rewriting Properties)

Characterization of Normal Forms.

Lemma 32
.

Let
a
be an answer. Then
\mathtt{shfv}(a)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Immediate induction on
a
.
∎

\gettoappendix

prop:weak-nfs

Proof.

Direction
\Rightarrow
. By induction on
t
. Cases:

•

Variable
or
abstraction
. Trivial.

•

Application
, that is,
t=su
. By
i.h.
,
s
belongs to the grammar. It cannot be a weak answer, otherwise
t
would have a
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}
redex. Then
s
is a inert term. By
i.h.
,
u
is a
n
term. Then
t
has shape
in
and belongs to the grammar.

•

Substitution
, that is,
t=s[x{\shortleftarrow}u]
. By
i.h.
,
s
belongs to the grammar. We have that
s
cannot be written as
W\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
otherwise there would be a
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
redex. Then
x\notin\mathtt{shfv}(u)
. By
i.h.
,
u
belongs to the grammar. Note that if
u
is a weak answer then it has shape
S\langle v\rangle
. Then
x\in\mathtt{fv}(u)
, otherwise there would be a
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
redex.

Direction
\Leftarrow
. By induction on
t
.

•

Variable
or
abstraction
. Trivial.

•

Application
, that is,
t=i^{\prime}n
. By
i.h.
, both
i
and
n
are
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
-normal. Moreover,
i
does not have shape
S\langle v\rangle
, so that the root application is not a
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}
redex. Thus
t
is
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}}
-normal.

•

Substitution
, that is,
t=s[x{\shortleftarrow}u]
. It follows from the
i.h.
and the conditions on
x
in the grammar, which forbid
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
-redexes because
x\notin\mathtt{shfv}(s)
and forbid
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
-redexes because them
u
is a weak answer then
x
is required to occur in
s
(necessarily under abstraction).∎

Postponement of GC by Value.

Lemma 33

(Local postponement of weak GC by value)
.

1.

Weak multiplicative
: if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}t^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}t^{\prime\prime}
then
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}t^{\prime\prime\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}t^{\prime\prime}
for some
t^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

2.

Weak exponential
: if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}t^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}t^{\prime\prime}
then
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}t^{\prime\prime\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}^{+}t^{\prime\prime}
for some
t^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

1.

Weak multiplicative
. By induction on
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}t^{\prime}
. Cases of
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}t^{\prime}
:

•

Root
, that is,
t=s[x{\shortleftarrow}S\langle v\rangle]\mapsto_{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}S\langle s\rangle
with
x\notin\mathtt{fv}(s)
. Cases of
S\langle s\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}t^{\prime\prime}
:

(a)

Step in
s
, that is,
S\langle s\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}S\langle s^{\prime}\rangle=t^{\prime\prime}
because
s\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}s^{\prime}
. Then:
s[x{\shortleftarrow}S\langle v\rangle]
S\langle s\rangle
s^{\prime}[x{\shortleftarrow}S\langle v\rangle]
S\langle s^{\prime}\rangle
\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}
\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}

\gettoappendix

prop:gc-postponement

Proof.

The statement is an instance of a well-known rewriting property: the local swaps in Lemma
33
.1-2 above can be put together as the following local postponement property:

t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}t^{\prime}\rightarrow_{\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}t^{\prime\prime}
then
t\rightarrow_{\neg\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}t^{\prime\prime\prime}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}^{*}t^{\prime\prime}
for some
t^{\prime\prime\prime}

which is an instance of Hindley’s strong postponement property, which implies the postponement property in the statement.
∎

\gettoappendix

prop:local-termination

Proof.

Strong normalization of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}
and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{gc}\mathtt{v}}
is trivial, since they decrease the number of constructors. Strong normalization of
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
follows from the fact that
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}
is an instance of the exponential rule
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}}
of the LSC, which is strongly normalizing, as proved in Accattoli
[
1
]
. ∎

A.1
Double Contexts

For the proof of confluence, we first need some definitions and technical tools to manage contexts, namely the outside-in order on contexts, contexts with
two
holes (called
double contexts
), and some of their properties.

Definition 34

(Outside-in context order)
.

We define the partial
outside-in
order
\prec
over weak contexts:

\langle\cdot\rangle\prec W

W\prec W^{\prime}

W^{\prime\prime}\langle W\rangle\prec W^{\prime\prime}\langle W^{\prime}\rangle

And say that
W
is
outer
than
W^{\prime}
if
W\prec W^{\prime}
. If
W\not\prec W^{\prime}
and
W^{\prime}\not\prec W
we say that
W
and
W^{\prime}
are
disjoint
, and write
W\parallel W^{\prime}
.

Double Contexts.

Double contexts have as base cases binary constructors (that is, applications and substitutions) having contexts replacing their subterms, and as inductive cases they are simply closed by an ordinary context.

Definition 35

(Double contexts)
.

Double contexts
\mathbb{W}
are defined by the following grammar.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\sep}llll}\textsc{Double contexts}\hfil\hskip 9.95863pt&\mathbb{W}&:=&WW^{\prime}\mid W[x{\shortleftarrow}W^{\prime}]\mid W\langle\mathbb{W}\rangle\end{array}

We now discuss some easy facts about double contexts.

•

Plugging
: the plugging
\mathbb{W}\langle t,s\rangle
of two terms
t
and
s
into a double context
\mathbb{W}
is defined as expected and gives a term. The two ways of plugging one term
\mathbb{W}\langle t,\langle\cdot\rangle\rangle
and
\mathbb{W}\langle\langle\cdot\rangle,s\rangle
into a double context give instead a context. We often use
\mathbb{W}\langle t,\cdot\rangle
and
\mathbb{W}\langle\cdot,s\rangle
as compact notations for
\mathbb{W}\langle t,\langle\cdot\rangle\rangle
and
\mathbb{W}\langle\langle\cdot\rangle,s\rangle
.

•

Pairs of disjoint positions and double contexts
: every pair of positions
W\langle t\rangle=W^{\prime}\langle s\rangle
which are disjoint, that is, such that
W\parallel W^{\prime}
, gives rise to a double context
\mathbb{W}_{W,W^{\prime}}
such that
\mathbb{W}_{W,W^{\prime}}\langle\cdot,s\rangle=W
and
\mathbb{W}_{W,W^{\prime}}\langle t,\cdot\rangle=W^{\prime}
. Conversely, every double context
\mathbb{W}
and every pair of terms
t
and
s
gives rise to two context
\mathbb{W}_{l}^{t,s}:=\mathbb{W}\langle\langle\cdot\rangle,s\rangle
and
W_{r}^{t,s}:=\mathbb{W}\langle t,\langle\cdot\rangle\rangle
. Note that the two constructions are inverse of each other, that is, given
\mathbb{W}
, we have
\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{W}_{l}^{t,s},W_{r}^{t,s}}=\mathbb{W}
, and, conversely, given
W\langle t\rangle=W^{\prime}\langle s\rangle
we have
(\mathbb{W}_{W,W^{\prime}})_{l}^{t,s}=W
and
(\mathbb{W}_{W,W^{\prime}})_{r}^{t,s}=W^{\prime}
.

Lemma 36
.

W\langle\mathbb{W}\langle t,\cdot\rangle\rangle=W\langle\mathbb{W}\rangle\langle t,\cdot\rangle
and
W\langle\mathbb{W}\langle\cdot,t\rangle\rangle=W\langle\mathbb{W}\rangle\langle\cdot,t\rangle
.

Proof.

By induction on
W
.
∎

A.2
Confluence

Lemma 37

(Deformation)
.

If
W\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{e}}s
then:

1.

either
s=W^{\prime}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
for some
W^{\prime}
such that
W\langle u\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}W^{\prime}\langle u\rangle
for every term
u
,

2.

or
W=W^{\prime}\langle u[y{\shortleftarrow}W^{\prime\prime}]\rangle
for some
u
,
W^{\prime}
, and
W^{\prime\prime}
, and the step in the hypothesis reduces a
\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}
-redex on
y
.

Proof.

By induction on
W\langle x\rangle\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}s
. Cases:

•

Root
, that is,
W\langle x\rangle=W^{\prime\prime\prime}\langle y\rangle[y{\shortleftarrow}r]\mapsto_{\mathtt{e}_{W}}W^{\prime\prime\prime}\langle r\rangle[y{\shortleftarrow}r]
. Cases:

–

The hole of
W
falls in
r
: then
r=W^{\prime\prime}\langle x\rangle
for some
W^{\prime\prime}
, we are in case 2 with
W^{\prime}:=\langle\cdot\rangle
and
u:=W^{\prime\prime\prime}\langle y\rangle
.

–

The hole of
W
falls in
W^{\prime\prime\prime}\langle y\rangle
: then
W^{\prime\prime\prime}\langle y\rangle=W^{\bullet}\langle x\rangle
for some
W^{\bullet}
, with
W=W^{\bullet}[y{\shortleftarrow}r]
. Since
W^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
W^{\bullet}
are disjoint, there is a double context
\mathbb{W}
such that
\mathbb{W}\langle x,\cdot\rangle=W^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
\mathbb{W}\langle\cdot,y\rangle=W^{\bullet}
. Then we are in case 1 with
W^{\prime}:=\mathbb{W}\langle\cdot,r\rangle[y{\shortleftarrow}r]
, as the step in the hypotheses writes as:

W\langle x\rangle=\mathbb{W}\langle x,y\rangle[y{\shortleftarrow}r]\mapsto_{\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{w}}}\mathbb{W}\langle x,r\rangle[y{\shortleftarrow}r]=W^{\prime}\langle x\rangle

and that moreover:

W\langle u\rangle=\mathbb{W}\langle u,y\rangle[y{\shortleftarrow}r]\mapsto_{\mathtt{e}_{\mathtt{w}}}\mathbb{W}\langle u,r\rangle[y{\shortleftarrow}r]=W^{\prime}\langle u\rangle
.

•

Left of an application
, that is,
t=su\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}s^{\prime}u=t^{\prime}
. Cases:

1.

The hole of
W
falls in
s
. Then it follows from the
i.h.

2.

The hole of
W
falls in
u
. Then
u=W^{\prime\prime\prime}\langle x\rangle
for some
W^{\prime\prime\prime}
. We are in case 1 with
W^{\prime}:=sW^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

•

Right of an application
, that is,
t=us\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}us^{\prime}=t^{\prime}
. Analogous to the previous case.

•

Left of a substitution
, that is,
t=s[y{\shortleftarrow}u]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}s^{\prime}[y{\shortleftarrow}u]=t^{\prime}
. Cases:

1.

The hole of
W
falls in
s
. Then it follows from the
i.h.

2.

The hole of
W
falls in
u
. Then
u=W^{\prime\prime\prime}\langle x\rangle
for some
W^{\prime\prime\prime}
. We are in case 1 with
W^{\prime}:=s[y{\shortleftarrow}W^{\prime\prime\prime}]
.

•

Right of a substitution
, that is,
t=u[y{\shortleftarrow}s]\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}u[y{\shortleftarrow}s^{\prime}]=t^{\prime}
. Analogous to the previous case.∎

\gettoappendix

l:local-confluence

1.

S\langle s[x{\shortleftarrow}u^{\prime}]\rangle
\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}
\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}
\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}
\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}

(a)

. Then it follows from the

(b)

(c)

, that is,
t=us\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{m}}us^{\prime}=t^{\prime}

. Then it follows from the

(b)

. Then the two steps are disjoint and trivially swap.

Right of a substitution
, that is,

2.

Exponential

t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}t^{\prime}
and

. By induction on
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}t^{\prime}
. Cases of
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}t^{\prime}
:

•

Root
, that is,
t=W\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}[x{\shortleftarrow}s]\mapsto_{\mathtt{e}_{W}}W\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}s\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-2.72221pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}[x{\shortleftarrow}s]
. Cases of
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{w}\mathtt{e}}t^{\prime\prime}
:

(a)

Step in
s
: then the diagram closes as follows::
